No changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a formal medical evaluation, such as a memory test or a cognitive screening, where a doctor looked into these lapses in memory?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't done anything like that. I mean, my regular doctor, Dr. Miller, he checks my blood pressure and cholesterol and all that normal stuff, but he's never given me a test for my memory. \n\nHis office is over on 4th Street now. They just moved out of that old brick building near the library. I kind of miss the old place, to be honest with you. It had these big oak doors and a waiting room that actually felt comfortable, not like these new clinics that look like spaceships with all the cold white plastic and glass everywhere. And they used to have decent magazines, too. National Geographic, Popular Mechanics... Now it's just pamphlets about eating healthy and a bunch of golf magazines. I don't even play golf. I tried it once back in the 80s with my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary was absolutely obsessed with it, bought all the expensive clubs, the weird plaid pants, the shoes with the little spikes on the bottom. We went out to that public course by the interstate, and I swear, I spent more time looking for my ball in the woods than actually hitting the darn thing. Plus, the mosquitoes out there were the size of small birds. I ended up with bites all over my ankles, and I told Gary, I said, \"You can keep your nine irons, I'm sticking to fishing.\" \n\nBut, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, a medical test for my memory. No, no one's ever had me do a screening or anything like that. Just the regular checkups.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year or so, would you say these lapses in memory and attention have been getting noticeably worse, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's just the past year. It's been noticeably causing trouble for... oh, about two years and four months now. It started right after my buddy Arnie passed away. He used to handle all the liaison duties for the historical society down at the museum, and when he died, I took it all over myself. \n\nArnie was a real history buff, you know? He had this massive collection of Civil War memorabilia in his basement. He had an actual cannonball—at least, he claimed it was an actual cannonball. It looked like a rusty bowling ball to me, but he swore he found it in a creek bed in Virginia when he was a kid. He used to use it as a doorstop for his den. His wife, Brenda, absolutely hated it. She was always stubbing her toe on the darn thing when she went in there to vacuum. Brenda was a meticulous cleaner. I remember one time she made us take our shoes off before we even walked onto the front porch, because they had just repainted the floorboards. It was this awful shade of seafoam green... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the museum duties. Yeah, since I took over all that by myself about two years and four months ago, that's when I really started noticing the trouble keeping track of things. It's just a lot to juggle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as any new tremors, stiffness in your muscles, or trouble with your balance while walking?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't had the shakes or any stiffness, and my balance is just fine. I still walk down to the post office every morning to get the mail. It's about a half-mile round trip, good for the legs. \n\nI usually run into Mrs. Gable on the way. She's got this little yappy dog, some kind of terrier mix, I think. Barks at every single leaf that blows across the sidewalk. I don't know how she stands it. My dog, Buster, he was a golden retriever. Best dog you could ever ask for. He wouldn't bark unless somebody was actually knocking on the front door, and even then, it was just one big woof to let you know. We used to take him up to the lake in the summers. He loved the water. He'd swim out after a tennis ball until he was practically exhausted. One time, he brought back a turtle instead of the ball. Just carried it right up onto the beach in his mouth, gentle as could be. The turtle wasn't hurt or anything, just looked a little confused. My daughter, she was about eight at the time, she thought it was the funniest thing in the world. She wanted to keep it in a shoebox, but I told her... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my health. Tremors and balance and stuff. No, no, physically I'm doing fine. Just walking the dog—well, walking myself now, I guess. No problems with my muscles or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory lapses or difficulties with your museum duties ever caused you to make significant errors in your work or led to complaints from the museum staff?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say there were complaints, exactly, but... yeah, the museum group has had to quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me. I used to organize the whole spring gala, you know. We'd have it catered by that Italian place on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it was called? No, wait, Luigi's was the shoe repair shop. It was... Mario's. Yes, Mario's. They made this incredible baked ziti. The secret was they used three different kinds of cheese, and they'd bake it until the edges got all crispy. I love those crispy edges. I tried making it at home once, but I left it in the oven too long because I was watching a documentary on the History Channel about the Hoover Dam. Did you know they poured so much concrete for that thing that if they did it all at once, it would have taken over a hundred years to cool down? They had to run miles of pipe through it with ice water just to get it to set right. Engineering is fascinating like that. My nephew, Kevin, he's studying engineering up at the state college. He wants to build bridges. I told him he better be good at math, because you don't want to be the guy who forgets to carry a one and then the whole bridge falls into the river. Kevin's a smart kid, though. He's always been good with numbers. \n\nBut, uh... where was I going with that? Oh, right, the museum. Yeah, they've taken some of the planning stuff off my plate. And I've stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I just... I lose my train of thought, and it's embarrassing when you've got ten people staring at you waiting for the history of a painting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality, such as becoming more irritable, apathetic, or perhaps acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my personality has changed much. I mean, I don't feel any more irritable or anything like that. I'm pretty easygoing, usually. Always have been. My wife, Mary, she used to say I was so laid back I was practically horizontal. She, on the other hand, she could get fired up over the smallest things. Like if the neighbor didn't trim their hedges just right. Our neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had these boxwood hedges along the property line, and he insisted on trimming them into these weird shapes. One of them looked exactly like a giant teapot. I thought it was kind of funny, but Mary hated it. She said it made the neighborhood look like a miniature golf course. We used to love playing mini golf when the kids were young. There was this great place out on Route 9 with a windmill and a pirate ship. I got a hole-in-one on the 18th hole once and won a free game. They gave me this little token... it was brass, I think. I kept it in my wallet for years. I wonder whatever happened to that token. I had to get a new wallet a few years ago because the leather on the old one completely wore through. You know, they just don't make things the way they used to. I bought a toaster last year, and the darn thing broke after three months. Just stopped popping the toast up. You had to stand there and watch it, or you'd end up with charcoal. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my personality. Right. No, no changes there. I'm still the same old me, just... maybe a little more forgetful, but I don't think I'm acting out of character or getting angry or anything.\n\nDoctor: Dr. Smith, aside from the routine checks for blood pressure and cholesterol, have you ever been diagnosed with any chronic medical conditions like diabetes, thyroid issues, or vitamin deficiencies?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any of that. No diabetes or thyroid problems, thank goodness. You mentioned vitamins, though—my wife used to buy these big multivitamin horse pills from the pharmacy for me. They smelled absolutely awful, kind of like fish food. Which is funny, because we used to have this big 50-gallon aquarium right in the living room when the kids were little. It was a beautiful setup. We had neon tetras, a couple of little catfish, and these two huge angelfish. We named one of the angelfish Captain Ahab because he was missing a little chunk out of his top fin. I think he scraped it on the plastic shipwreck we had in the corner of the tank. You know, it is amazingly relaxing to just sit and watch fish swim around. I could sit there for an hour with my morning coffee just watching them go back and forth. I actually tried to set up a smaller tank again a couple of years ago, but the water pump kept making this terrible rattling noise, and I couldn't get the filter to sit right on the glass, so I ended up just packing it all back into the box. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my health. Diabetes and all that. No, no, I don't have any of those chronic conditions. Just the regular checkups, but I'm pretty healthy in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your depth perception, such as misjudging the distance of steps or having trouble parking your car?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My parking is still pretty good. I drive one of those big old Buick LeSabres, you know, the ones that feel like you're steering a boat. The guy who sold it to me years ago, he was a real character. He had this bright yellow tie with little purple hippos all over it. I remember asking him about it, and he said his daughter picked it out for Father's Day. Father's Day is always so tough to buy for. My kids usually just get me a gift card to Home Depot, which is fine by me, honestly. I could spend hours just wandering around the hardware store. They have that whole section with the riding lawn mowers right when you walk in. I always wanted a riding mower, but our yard isn't nearly big enough to justify it. It would take me longer to back the thing out of the shed than it would to just push my old manual mower across the lawn. Although, the grass has been growing so fast lately with all the rain we've had. It rained for what, three days straight last week? The gutters were completely overflowing. I really need to get up on a ladder and clean those out before the fall leaves start dropping, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about getting on the ladder? Oh, steps! Right, and parking. No, no trouble with judging distances or anything like that. My vision is pretty good in that regard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, yeah, I handle all the bills myself. No problem there. I still balance my checkbook by hand every month, too. I know most people do it all on the computer or their phones now, but I like having the paper record. I actually just ordered a new box of checks last week from the bank. I actually tried to set up a smaller tank again a couple of years ago, but the water pump kept making this terrible rattling noise, and I couldn't get the filter to sit right on the glass, so I ended up just packing it all back into the box. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my health. Diabetes and all that. No, no, I don't have any of those chronic conditions. Just the regular checkups, but I'm pretty healthy in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your depth perception, such as misjudging the distance of steps or having trouble parking your car?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My parking is still pretty good. I drive one of those big old Buick LeSabres, you know, the ones that feel like you're steering a boat. The guy who sold it to me years ago, he was a real character. He had this bright yellow tie with little purple hippos all over it. I remember asking him about it, and he said his daughter picked it out for Father's Day. Father's Day is always so tough to buy for. My kids usually just get me a gift card to Home Depot, which is fine by me, honestly. I could spend hours just wandering around the hardware store. They have that whole section with the riding lawn mowers right when you walk in. I always wanted a riding mower, but our yard isn't nearly big enough to justify it. It would take me longer to back the thing out of the shed than it would to just push my old manual mower across the lawn. Although, the grass has been growing so fast lately with all the rain we've had. It rained for what, three days straight last week? The gutters were completely overflowing. I really need to get up on a ladder and clean those out before the fall leaves start dropping, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about getting on the ladder? Oh, steps! Right, and parking. No, no trouble with judging distances or anything like that. My vision is pretty good in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently able to manage all of your own personal finances, such as paying your monthly bills and balancing your checkbook, without any assistance from others?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, I handle all the bills myself. No problem there. I still balance my checkbook by hand every month, too. I know most people do it all on the computer or their phones now, but I like having the paper record. I actually just ordered a new box of checks last week from the bank. I always get the ones with the little classic cars printed on them. The last batch had a '57 Chevy on it. Beautiful car. My uncle had a '57 Chevy, cherry red. He used to take us for drives out in the country on Sundays when I was a kid. We'd always stop at this little roadside stand that sold boiled peanuts and the absolute best peach ice cream you've ever had. You just can't find ice cream like that in the grocery store anymore. It was so rich, it would melt all over your hands before you could even finish the cone. I remember dropping a whole scoop on the back seat of that Chevy once, and my uncle was so mad... he spent an hour scrubbing the upholstery with club soda. Club soda really is a miracle worker for stains, though. I used it just the other day when I spilled my morning coffee on the living room rug. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh! My finances. Right. Yes, I pay all my own bills and manage the checkbook. I don't need any help with that, I do it all myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Club soda really is a miracle worker for stains, though. I used it just the other day when I spilled my morning coffee on the living room rug. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh! My finances. Right. Yes, I pay all my own bills and manage the checkbook. I don't need any help with that, I do it all myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a brief cognitive screening test, like a short list of words to remember or a simple drawing task, done by a healthcare professional?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that done. Nobody's ever asked me to remember a list of words or draw anything. Well, I mean, my granddaughter asked me to draw a horse for her last weekend. She's five, and she is absolutely obsessed with horses right now. Everything is ponies and unicorns. I tried to replace the battery, but the compartment is screwed shut with one of those tiny little Phillips head screws, and I couldn't find my precision screwdriver set. I know I put it in the top drawer of the workbench in the garage, but my son borrowed some tools a few weeks ago to fix his lawnmower, and you know how it is—things never make it back to where they belong. He's always been like that, even when he was a teenager. His room was a disaster zone. I used to tell him he was going to find a new species of mold growing under his bed one day... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Drawing? Oh, right, the medical tests. No, no doctor has ever given me a memory test or made me draw anything. Just the regular physical stuff.\n\nDoctor: Since you have been noticing these memory lapses, have you found that you are unable to complete your daily chores or personal tasks as effectively as you used to, even if you are still technically able to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unable to do them. I mean, I still get everything done around the house. The chores get finished, eventually. Like yesterday, I was supposed to be doing the laundry. I got the whites sorted out and put them in the wash, but then I noticed the washing machine was making this... this awful squeaking noise during the spin cycle. The guy who finally showed up was this huge guy named Tiny, which I always think is such a funny nickname for a big guy. He told me he used to be a professional wrestler on the local circuit before he blew out his knee. Said his finishing move was called the \"Tiny Terror.\" Anyway, I started looking for my toolbox to see if I could tighten the belt on the washer, and that's when I realized I still hadn't found that precision screwdriver set from the other day. The ones with the little hand-painted snowmen. She was so happy when I brought those in... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Oh, the chores. Right. No, I'm still doing all my chores and tasks just fine. It might take me a little longer sometimes because I get sidetracked, but I still get them all done. I'm not unable to do them or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I don't need anyone's help. I can manage everything on my own just fine. I still do all the cooking and the cleaning, and I take care of the yard. Well, I mean, the yard is a bit of a project right now. I was out there the other day trying to trim the hedges along the fence line, but my electric clippers gave out on me. Just completely died. Completely ruined the outdoor watercolor displays, which I told them would happen because November weather here is just so unpredictable... \n\nBut anyway, what brings me in is that people at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking right at them, but then... I suddenly realize I missed the whole middle of what they just said. I have to stop them and ask them to run it by me again. It's getting embarrassing. Especially with Mary, the coordinator, because she talks so fast to begin with and she always wears these loud clunky bracelets that just clack against the glass cases when she points at things, so it's already hard enough to follow her... but yeah, I just keep missing chunks of conversations.\n\nDoctor: When you have these moments where you miss what is being said, do you ever feel as though you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels dreamlike or unreal?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing weird like that. I had this '78 C10 back in my twenties, beautiful two-tone blue and white. I kept that thing running for almost fifteen years until the transmission finally gave out on me right on Route 9. Had to walk two miles in the pitch dark to find a payphone. You remember payphones, right? It's a real shame they got rid of them all. Now everybody's just staring at their cell phones all day. Oh, right, the out-of-body thing. No, I don't get anything like that. I'm completely awake, I just sort of... drift off and miss the words.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these moments where you drift off during conversations, would you say that you spend a significant amount of time feeling worried or anxious about various things in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I wouldn't call myself a worrier. I pretty much just take things one day at a time since I stopped working. I mean, I have my little routines to keep me busy. Like my yard, for instance. I spend a lot of time out there, and I just put in these new beefsteak tomatoes. I had to plant them late this year because the local hardware store—the one over on Elm, not the big chain store, I hate going to those big box places. You can never find anybody to help you, and when you finally do, it's some teenager who doesn't know a Phillips from a flathead... \n\nBut anyway, they were completely out of the good potting soil. The kind with the moisture control? If you don't use that stuff around here, the summer heat just bakes the roots right out. My neighbor Frank never uses it, and his lawn looks like a dried-up sponge by August. He's always out there with his sprinkler at high noon, which is just about the worst time you can water, because the sun just evaporates it all before it even hits the dirt... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, anxiety. No, I don't really get anxious or stressed about things. Life's too short to spend it worrying.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these moments where you lose the thread of a conversation, have you noticed any other changes in your day-to-day life, such as difficulty keeping track of appointments, forgetting where you've put things, or struggling to manage tasks you used to do easily?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you mention it, keeping track of things has been... a bit of a headache lately. Especially down at the museum. I'm always putting down the camera battery, or my volunteer name tag, or that big manila envelope with all the sign-up sheets. I'll tell myself, \"I'm putting this right here by the front desk so it's safe,\" and then twenty minutes later, I'm spending half the morning retracing my steps trying to find the darn things. \n\nIt reminds me of this time my brother lost his car keys at the county fair. We must have walked the entire midway three times looking for them. And let me tell you, that midway is huge. They always set it up right next to the livestock barns, which, if the wind is blowing the wrong way, makes the whole funnel cake experience a lot less appealing. I used to love those funnel cakes, though. The lady who ran the stand used this specific type of powdered sugar that just melted the second it hit the hot dough. I tried making them at home once, but my deep fryer—well, it's one of those cheap ones you get on sale after Thanksgiving—it just couldn't get the oil hot enough. Ended up with a soggy, greasy mess that even the dog wouldn't eat. And Buster eats everything. He once ate an entire tube of toothpaste... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, misplacing things. Yeah, it's just little things like the sign-up sheets or my keys. I just keep putting them down and forgetting where they went.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform complex tasks, such as managing your finances, preparing meals, or using your telephone?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any trouble with that sort of thing. I pay the bills the day they come in, and I still cook all my own meals just fine. Actually, I just made a big batch of chili yesterday. I use this specific chili powder I have to order from a little place down in Texas. My buddy Arnie told me about it years ago. \n\nArnie and I used to go fishing down at the reservoir every spring before the county blocked off the main access road. It's a real shame they did that, because the bass fishing there was just incredible. We'd sit out there on the bank for hours. Arnie had this old metal tackle box that was rusted shut on one side, so he always had to pry it open with his pocket knife. He carried that knife everywhere. I think it was one of those Swiss Army ones, you know, the kind with the little scissors that never actually cut anything. I bought one for my nephew once for his birthday, but his mother took it away because she thought he'd poke his eye out. She was always overprotective like that, even when we were kids... \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, managing things. No, no problems there. Bills, cooking, the phone—it's all fine. I mean, I hate typing on that little glass screen on the smartphone my daughter got me, but I know how to use it. No changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a formal medical evaluation, such as a memory test or a cognitive screening, where a doctor looked into these lapses in memory?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't done anything like that. I mean, my regular doctor, Dr. Miller, he checks my blood pressure and cholesterol and all that normal stuff, but he's never given me a test for my memory. \n\nHis office is over on 4th Street now. They just moved out of that old brick building near the library. I kind of miss the old place, to be honest with you. It had these big oak doors and a waiting room that actually felt comfortable, not like these new clinics that look like spaceships with all the cold white plastic and glass everywhere. And they used to have decent magazines, too. National Geographic, Popular Mechanics... Now it's just pamphlets about eating healthy and a bunch of golf magazines. I don't even play golf. I tried it once back in the 80s with my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary was absolutely obsessed with it, bought all the expensive clubs, the weird plaid pants, the shoes with the little spikes on the bottom. We went out to that public course by the interstate, and I swear, I spent more time looking for my ball in the woods than actually hitting the darn thing. Plus, the mosquitoes out there were the size of small birds. Oh, right, a medical test for my memory. No, no one's ever had me do a screening or anything like that. Just the regular checkups.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year or so, would you say these lapses in memory and attention have been getting noticeably worse, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's just the past year. It's been noticeably causing trouble for... oh, about two years and four months now. It started right after my buddy Arnie passed away. He used to handle all the liaison duties for the historical society down at the museum, and when he died, I took it all over myself. \n\nArnie was a real history buff, you know? He had this massive collection of Civil War memorabilia in his basement. He had an actual cannonball—at least, he claimed it was an actual cannonball. It looked like a rusty bowling ball to me, but he swore he found it in a creek bed in Virginia when he was a kid. He used to use it as a doorstop for his den. His wife, Brenda, absolutely hated it. She was always stubbing her toe on the darn thing when she went in there to vacuum. Brenda was a meticulous cleaner. I remember one time she made us take our shoes off before we even walked onto the front porch, because they had just repainted the floorboards. It was this awful shade of seafoam green... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the museum duties. Yeah, since I took over all that by myself about two years and four months ago, that's when I really started noticing the trouble keeping track of things. It's just a lot to juggle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as any new tremors, stiffness in your muscles, or trouble with your balance while walking?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't had the shakes or any stiffness, and my balance is just fine. I still walk down to the post office every morning to get the mail. It's about a half-mile round trip, good for the legs. \n\nI usually run into Mrs. Gable on the way. She's got this little yappy dog, some kind of terrier mix, I think. Barks at every single leaf that blows across the sidewalk. I don't know how she stands it. My dog, Buster, he was a golden retriever. Best dog you could ever ask for. He wouldn't bark unless somebody was actually knocking on the front door, and even then, it was just one big woof to let you know. We used to take him up to the lake in the summers. He loved the water. He'd swim out after a tennis ball until he was practically exhausted. One time, he brought back a turtle instead of the ball. Just carried it right up onto the beach in his mouth, gentle as could be. The turtle wasn't hurt or anything, just looked a little confused. My daughter, she was about eight at the time, she thought it was the funniest thing in the world. She wanted to keep it in a shoebox, but I told her... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my health. Tremors and balance and stuff. No, no, physically I'm doing fine. Just walking the dog—well, walking myself now, I guess. No problems with my muscles or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory lapses or difficulties with your museum duties ever caused you to make significant errors in your work or led to complaints from the museum staff?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say there were complaints, exactly, but... yeah, the museum group has had to quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me. I used to organize the whole spring gala, you know. We'd have it catered by that Italian place on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it was called? No, wait, Luigi's was the shoe repair shop. It was... Mario's. Yes, Mario's. They made this incredible baked ziti. The secret was they used three different kinds of cheese, and they'd bake it until the edges got all crispy. I love those crispy edges. I tried making it at home once, but I left it in the oven too long because I was watching a documentary on the History Channel about the Hoover Dam. Did you know they poured so much concrete for that thing that if they did it all at once, it would have taken over a hundred years to cool down? They had to run miles of pipe through it with ice water just to get it to set right. Engineering is fascinating like that. My nephew, Kevin, he's studying engineering up at the state college. He wants to build bridges. I told him he better be good at math, because you don't want to be the guy who forgets to carry a one and then the whole bridge falls into the river. Kevin's a smart kid, though. He's always been good with numbers. \n\nBut, uh... where was I going with that? Oh, right, the museum. Yeah, they've taken some of the planning stuff off my plate. And I've stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I just... I lose my train of thought, and it's embarrassing when you've got ten people staring at you waiting for the history of a painting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality, such as becoming more irritable, apathetic, or perhaps acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my personality has changed much. I mean, I don't feel any more irritable or anything like that. I'm pretty easygoing, usually. Always have been. My wife, Mary, she used to say I was so laid back I was practically horizontal. She, on the other hand, she could get fired up over the smallest things. Like if the neighbor didn't trim their hedges just right. Our neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had these boxwood hedges along the property line, and he insisted on trimming them into these weird shapes. One of them looked exactly like a giant teapot. I thought it was kind of funny, but Mary hated it. She said it made the neighborhood look like a miniature golf course. We used to love playing mini golf when the kids were young. There was this great place out on Route 9 with a windmill and a pirate ship. I got a hole-in-one on the 18th hole once and won a free game. They gave me this little token... it was brass, I think. I kept it in my wallet for years. I wonder whatever happened to that token. I had to get a new wallet a few years ago because the leather on the old one completely wore through. I don't think I'd have a problem with that. Counting backward or remembering words? Sure, I could do that. I mean, my memory isn't what it used to be, but I'm not totally gone. I remember back in high school, Mr. Peterson—he was my math teacher, real strict guy, always wore these short-sleeved button-down shirts with a tie, even in the dead of winter. I used to have this dark blue corduroy jacket I wore every day, and it would just get covered in white dust. My mom was always getting onto me about it. She was a stickler for keeping things neat. Especially with Mary, the coordinator, because she talks so fast to begin with and she always wears these loud clunky bracelets that just clack against the glass cases when she points at things, so it's already hard enough to follow her... but yeah, I just keep missing chunks of conversations.\n\nDoctor: When you have these moments where you miss what is being said, do you ever feel as though you are observing yourself from outside your body, or that the world around you feels dreamlike or unreal?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing weird like that. Had to walk two miles in the pitch dark to find a payphone. You remember payphones, right? It's a real shame they got rid of them all. Now everybody's just staring at their cell phones all day. I wouldn't call myself a worrier. I pretty much just take things one day at a time since I stopped working. I mean, I have my little routines to keep me busy. Like my yard, for instance. I spend a lot of time out there, and I just put in these new beefsteak tomatoes. I had to plant them late this year because the local hardware store—the one over on Elm, not the big chain store, I hate going to those big box places. You can never find anybody to help you, and when you finally do, it's some teenager who doesn't know a Phillips from a flathead... \n\nBut anyway, they were completely out of the good potting soil. The kind with the moisture control? If you don't use that stuff around here, the summer heat just bakes the roots right out. My neighbor Frank never uses it, and his lawn looks like a dried-up sponge by August. He's always out there with his sprinkler at high noon, which is just about the worst time you can water, because the sun just evaporates it all before it even hits the dirt... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, anxiety. No, I don't really get anxious or stressed about things. Life's too short to spend it worrying.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these moments where you lose the thread of a conversation, have you noticed any other changes in your day-to-day life, such as difficulty keeping track of appointments, forgetting where you've put things, or struggling to manage tasks you used to do easily?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you mention it, keeping track of things has been... a bit of a headache lately. Especially down at the museum. I'm always putting down the camera battery, or my volunteer name tag, or that big manila envelope with all the sign-up sheets. I'll tell myself, \"I'm putting this right here by the front desk so it's safe,\" and then twenty minutes later, I'm spending half the morning retracing my steps trying to find the darn things. \n\nIt reminds me of this time my brother lost his car keys at the county fair. We must have walked the entire midway three times looking for them. And let me tell you, that midway is huge. They always set it up right next to the livestock barns, which, if the wind is blowing the wrong way, makes the whole funnel cake experience a lot less appealing. I used to love those funnel cakes, though. The lady who ran the stand used this specific type of powdered sugar that just melted the second it hit the hot dough. I tried making them at home once, but my deep fryer—well, it's one of those cheap ones you get on sale after Thanksgiving—it just couldn't get the oil hot enough. Ended up with a soggy, greasy mess that even the dog wouldn't eat. And Buster eats everything. He once ate an entire tube of toothpaste... \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, right, misplacing things. Yeah, it's just little things like the sign-up sheets or my keys. I just keep putting them down and forgetting where they went.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to perform complex tasks, such as managing your finances, preparing meals, or using your telephone?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any trouble with that sort of thing. I pay the bills the day they come in, and I still cook all my own meals just fine. Actually, I just made a big batch of chili yesterday. I use this specific chili powder I have to order from a little place down in Texas. My buddy Arnie told me about it years ago. \n\nArnie and I used to go fishing down at the reservoir every spring before the county blocked off the main access road. It's a real shame they did that, because the bass fishing there was just incredible. We'd sit out there on the bank for hours. Arnie had this old metal tackle box that was rusted shut on one side, so he always had to pry it open with his pocket knife. He carried that knife everywhere. I think it was one of those Swiss Army ones, you know, the kind with the little scissors that never actually cut anything. I bought one for my nephew once for his birthday, but his mother took it away because she thought he'd poke his eye out. She was always overprotective like that, even when we were kids... \n\nBut, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, managing things. No, no problems there. Bills, cooking, the phone—it's all fine. I mean, I hate typing on that little glass screen on the smartphone my daughter got me, but I know how to use it. No changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a formal medical evaluation, such as a memory test or a cognitive screening, where a doctor looked into these lapses in memory?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't done anything like that. I mean, my regular doctor, Dr. Miller, he checks my blood pressure and cholesterol and all that normal stuff, but he's never given me a test for my memory. \n\nHis office is over on 4th Street now. They just moved out of that old brick building near the library. I kind of miss the old place, to be honest with you. It had these big oak doors and a waiting room that actually felt comfortable, not like these new clinics that look like spaceships with all the cold white plastic and glass everywhere. And they used to have decent magazines, too. National Geographic, Popular Mechanics... Now it's just pamphlets about eating healthy and a bunch of golf magazines. I don't even play golf. I tried it once back in the 80s with my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary was absolutely obsessed with it, bought all the expensive clubs, the weird plaid pants, the shoes with the little spikes on the bottom. We went out to that public course by the interstate, and I swear, I spent more time looking for my ball in the woods than actually hitting the darn thing. Plus, the mosquitoes out there were the size of small birds. I ended up with bites all over my ankles, and I told Gary, I said, \"You can keep your nine irons, I'm sticking to fishing.\" \n\nBut, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, a medical test for my memory. No, no one's ever had me do a screening or anything like that. Just the regular checkups.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year or so, would you say these lapses in memory and attention have been getting noticeably worse, or have they stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's just the past year. It's been noticeably causing trouble for... oh, about two years and four months now. It started right after my buddy Arnie passed away. He used to handle all the liaison duties for the historical society down at the museum, and when he died, I took it all over myself. \n\nArnie was a real history buff, you know? He had this massive collection of Civil War memorabilia in his basement. He had an actual cannonball—at least, he claimed it was an actual cannonball. It looked like a rusty bowling ball to me, but he swore he found it in a creek bed in Virginia when he was a kid. He used to use it as a doorstop for his den. His wife, Brenda, absolutely hated it. She was always stubbing her toe on the darn thing when she went in there to vacuum. Brenda was a meticulous cleaner. I remember one time she made us take our shoes off before we even walked onto the front porch, because they had just repainted the floorboards. It was this awful shade of seafoam green... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the museum duties. Yeah, since I took over all that by myself about two years and four months ago, that's when I really started noticing the trouble keeping track of things. It's just a lot to juggle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as any new tremors, stiffness in your muscles, or trouble with your balance while walking?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that. I haven't had the shakes or any stiffness, and my balance is just fine. I still walk down to the post office every morning to get the mail. It's about a half-mile round trip, good for the legs. \n\nI usually run into Mrs. Gable on the way. She's got this little yappy dog, some kind of terrier mix, I think. Barks at every single leaf that blows across the sidewalk. I don't know how she stands it. My dog, Buster, he was a golden retriever. Best dog you could ever ask for. He wouldn't bark unless somebody was actually knocking on the front door, and even then, it was just one big woof to let you know. We used to take him up to the lake in the summers. He loved the water. He'd swim out after a tennis ball until he was practically exhausted. One time, he brought back a turtle instead of the ball. Just carried it right up onto the beach in his mouth, gentle as could be. The turtle wasn't hurt or anything, just looked a little confused. My daughter, she was about eight at the time, she thought it was the funniest thing in the world. She wanted to keep it in a shoebox, but I told her... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my health. Tremors and balance and stuff. No, no, physically I'm doing fine. Just walking the dog—well, walking myself now, I guess. No problems with my muscles or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory lapses or difficulties with your museum duties ever caused you to make significant errors in your work or led to complaints from the museum staff?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say there were complaints, exactly, but... yeah, the museum group has had to quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me. I used to organize the whole spring gala, you know. We'd have it catered by that Italian place on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it was called? No, wait, Luigi's was the shoe repair shop. It was... Mario's. Yes, Mario's. They made this incredible baked ziti. The secret was they used three different kinds of cheese, and they'd bake it until the edges got all crispy. I love those crispy edges. I tried making it at home once, but I left it in the oven too long because I was watching a documentary on the History Channel about the Hoover Dam. Did you know they poured so much concrete for that thing that if they did it all at once, it would have taken over a hundred years to cool down? They had to run miles of pipe through it with ice water just to get it to set right. Engineering is fascinating like that. My nephew, Kevin, he's studying engineering up at the state college. He wants to build bridges. I told him he better be good at math, because you don't want to be the guy who forgets to carry a one and then the whole bridge falls into the river. Kevin's a smart kid, though. He's always been good with numbers. \n\nBut, uh... where was I going with that? Oh, right, the museum. Yeah, they've taken some of the planning stuff off my plate. And I've stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I just... I lose my train of thought, and it's embarrassing when you've got ten people staring at you waiting for the history of a painting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality, such as becoming more irritable, apathetic, or perhaps acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my personality has changed much. I mean, I don't feel any more irritable or anything like that. I'm pretty easygoing, usually. Always have been. My wife, Mary, she used to say I was so laid back I was practically horizontal. She, on the other hand, she could get fired up over the smallest things. Like if the neighbor didn't trim their hedges just right. Our neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had these boxwood hedges along the property line, and he insisted on trimming them into these weird shapes. One of them looked exactly like a giant teapot. I thought it was kind of funny, but Mary hated it. She said it made the neighborhood look like a miniature golf course. We used to love playing mini golf when the kids were young. There was this great place out on Route 9 with a windmill and a pirate ship. I got a hole-in-one on the 18th hole once and won a free game. They gave me this little token... it was brass, I think. I kept it in my wallet for years. I wonder whatever happened to that token. I had to get a new wallet a few years ago because the leather on the old one completely wore through. You know, they just don't make things the way they used to. I bought a toaster last year, and the darn thing broke after three months. Just stopped popping the toast up. You had to stand there and watch it, or you'd end up with charcoal. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my personality. Right. No, no changes there. I'm still the same old me, just... maybe a little more forgetful, but I don't think I'm acting out of character or getting angry or anything.\n\nDoctor: Dr. Smith, aside from the routine checks for blood pressure and cholesterol, have you ever been diagnosed with any chronic medical conditions like diabetes, thyroid issues, or vitamin deficiencies?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any of that. No diabetes or thyroid problems, thank goodness. You mentioned vitamins, though—my wife used to buy these big multivitamin horse pills from the pharmacy for me. They smelled absolutely awful, kind of like fish food. Which is funny, because we used to have this big 50-gallon aquarium right in the living room when the kids were little. It was a beautiful setup. We had neon tetras, a couple of little catfish, and these two huge angelfish. We named one of the angelfish Captain Ahab because he was missing a little chunk out of his top fin. I think he scraped it on the plastic shipwreck we had in the corner of the tank. You know, it is amazingly relaxing to just sit and watch fish swim around. I could sit there for an hour with my morning coffee just watching them go back and forth. I actually tried to set up a smaller tank again a couple of years ago, but the water pump kept making this terrible rattling noise, and I couldn't get the filter to sit right on the glass, so I ended up just packing it all back into the box. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my health. Diabetes and all that. No, no, I don't have any of those chronic conditions. Just the regular checkups, but I'm pretty healthy in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your depth perception, such as misjudging the distance of steps or having trouble parking your car?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My parking is still pretty good. I drive one of those big old Buick LeSabres, you know, the ones that feel like you're steering a boat. The guy who sold it to me years ago, he was a real character. He had this bright yellow tie with little purple hippos all over it. I remember asking him about it, and he said his daughter picked it out for Father's Day. Father's Day is always so tough to buy for. My kids usually just get me a gift card to Home Depot, which is fine by me, honestly. I could spend hours just wandering around the hardware store. They have that whole section with the riding lawn mowers right when you walk in. I always wanted a riding mower, but our yard isn't nearly big enough to justify it. It would take me longer to back the thing out of the shed than it would to just push my old manual mower across the lawn. Right, and parking. No, no trouble with judging distances or anything like that. My vision is pretty good in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently able to manage all of your own personal finances, such as paying your monthly bills and balancing your checkbook, without any assistance from others?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, I handle all the bills myself. I actually just ordered a new box of checks last week from the bank. I always get the ones with the little classic cars printed on them. The last batch had a '57 Chevy on it. Beautiful car. We'd always stop at this little roadside stand that sold boiled peanuts and the absolute best peach ice cream you've ever had. You just can't find ice cream like that in the grocery store anymore. It's about a half-mile round trip, good for the legs. \n\nI usually run into Mrs. Gable on the way. She's got this little yappy dog, some kind of terrier mix, I think. Barks at every single leaf that blows across the sidewalk. I don't know how she stands it. My dog, Buster, he was a golden retriever. Best dog you could ever ask for. He wouldn't bark unless somebody was actually knocking on the front door, and even then, it was just one big woof to let you know. We used to take him up to the lake in the summers. He loved the water. He'd swim out after a tennis ball until he was practically exhausted. One time, he brought back a turtle instead of the ball. Just carried it right up onto the beach in his mouth, gentle as could be. The turtle wasn't hurt or anything, just looked a little confused. My daughter, she was about eight at the time, she thought it was the funniest thing in the world. She wanted to keep it in a shoebox, but I told her... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my health. Tremors and balance and stuff. No, no, physically I'm doing fine. Just walking the dog—well, walking myself now, I guess. No problems with my muscles or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory lapses or difficulties with your museum duties ever caused you to make significant errors in your work or led to complaints from the museum staff?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say there were complaints, exactly, but... yeah, the museum group has had to quietly shift some of the planning pieces away from me. I used to organize the whole spring gala, you know. We'd have it catered by that Italian place on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it was called? No, wait, Luigi's was the shoe repair shop. It was... Mario's. Yes, Mario's. They made this incredible baked ziti. The secret was they used three different kinds of cheese, and they'd bake it until the edges got all crispy. I love those crispy edges. I tried making it at home once, but I left it in the oven too long because I was watching a documentary on the History Channel about the Hoover Dam. Did you know they poured so much concrete for that thing that if they did it all at once, it would have taken over a hundred years to cool down? They had to run miles of pipe through it with ice water just to get it to set right. Engineering is fascinating like that. My nephew, Kevin, he's studying engineering up at the state college. He wants to build bridges. I told him he better be good at math, because you don't want to be the guy who forgets to carry a one and then the whole bridge falls into the river. Kevin's a smart kid, though. He's always been good with numbers. \n\nBut, uh... where was I going with that? Oh, right, the museum. Yeah, they've taken some of the planning stuff off my plate. And I've stopped feeling confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I just... I lose my train of thought, and it's embarrassing when you've got ten people staring at you waiting for the history of a painting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personality, such as becoming more irritable, apathetic, or perhaps acting in ways that seem out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think my personality has changed much. I mean, I don't feel any more irritable or anything like that. I'm pretty easygoing, usually. Always have been. My wife, Mary, she used to say I was so laid back I was practically horizontal. She, on the other hand, she could get fired up over the smallest things. Like if the neighbor didn't trim their hedges just right. Our neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had these boxwood hedges along the property line, and he insisted on trimming them into these weird shapes. One of them looked exactly like a giant teapot. I thought it was kind of funny, but Mary hated it. She said it made the neighborhood look like a miniature golf course. We used to love playing mini golf when the kids were young. There was this great place out on Route 9 with a windmill and a pirate ship. I got a hole-in-one on the 18th hole once and won a free game. They gave me this little token... it was brass, I think. I kept it in my wallet for years. I wonder whatever happened to that token. I had to get a new wallet a few years ago because the leather on the old one completely wore through. You know, they just don't make things the way they used to. I bought a toaster last year, and the darn thing broke after three months. Just stopped popping the toast up. You had to stand there and watch it, or you'd end up with charcoal. \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my personality. Right. No, no changes there. I'm still the same old me, just... maybe a little more forgetful, but I don't think I'm acting out of character or getting angry or anything.\n\nDoctor: Dr. Smith, aside from the routine checks for blood pressure and cholesterol, have you ever been diagnosed with any chronic medical conditions like diabetes, thyroid issues, or vitamin deficiencies?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any of that. No diabetes or thyroid problems, thank goodness. You mentioned vitamins, though—my wife used to buy these big multivitamin horse pills from the pharmacy for me. They smelled absolutely awful, kind of like fish food. Which is funny, because we used to have this big 50-gallon aquarium right in the living room when the kids were little. It was a beautiful setup. We had neon tetras, a couple of little catfish, and these two huge angelfish. We named one of the angelfish Captain Ahab because he was missing a little chunk out of his top fin. I think he scraped it on the plastic shipwreck we had in the corner of the tank. You know, it is amazingly relaxing to just sit and watch fish swim around. I could sit there for an hour with my morning coffee just watching them go back and forth. I actually tried to set up a smaller tank again a couple of years ago, but the water pump kept making this terrible rattling noise, and I couldn't get the filter to sit right on the glass, so I ended up just packing it all back into the box. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my health. Diabetes and all that. No, no, I don't have any of those chronic conditions. Just the regular checkups, but I'm pretty healthy in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your depth perception, such as misjudging the distance of steps or having trouble parking your car?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. My parking is still pretty good. I drive one of those big old Buick LeSabres, you know, the ones that feel like you're steering a boat. The guy who sold it to me years ago, he was a real character. He had this bright yellow tie with little purple hippos all over it. I remember asking him about it, and he said his daughter picked it out for Father's Day. Father's Day is always so tough to buy for. My kids usually just get me a gift card to Home Depot, which is fine by me, honestly. I could spend hours just wandering around the hardware store. They have that whole section with the riding lawn mowers right when you walk in. I always wanted a riding mower, but our yard isn't nearly big enough to justify it. It would take me longer to back the thing out of the shed than it would to just push my old manual mower across the lawn. Although, the grass has been growing so fast lately with all the rain we've had. It rained for what, three days straight last week? The gutters were completely overflowing. I really need to get up on a ladder and clean those out before the fall leaves start dropping, but... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about getting on the ladder? Oh, steps! Right, and parking. No, no trouble with judging distances or anything like that. My vision is pretty good in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently able to manage all of your own personal finances, such as paying your monthly bills and balancing your checkbook, without any assistance from others?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, I handle all the bills myself. No problem there. I still balance my checkbook by hand every month, too. I know most people do it all on the computer or their phones now, but I like having the paper record. I actually just ordered a new box of checks last week from the bank. I always get the ones with the little classic cars printed on them. The last batch had a '57 Chevy on it. Beautiful car. My uncle had a '57 Chevy, cherry red. He used to take us for drives out in the country on Sundays when I was a kid. We'd always stop at this little roadside stand that sold boiled peanuts and the absolute best peach ice cream you've ever had. You just can't find ice cream like that in the grocery store anymore. It was so rich, it would melt all over your hands before you could even finish the cone. I remember dropping a whole scoop on the back seat of that Chevy once, and my uncle was so mad... he spent an hour scrubbing the upholstery with club soda. Club soda really is a miracle worker for stains, though. I used it just the other day when I spilled my morning coffee on the living room rug. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh! My finances. Right. Yes, I pay all my own bills and manage the checkbook. I don't need any help with that, I do it all myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a brief cognitive screening test, like a short list of words to remember or a simple drawing task, done by a healthcare professional?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that done. Nobody's ever asked me to remember a list of words or draw anything. Well, I mean, my granddaughter asked me to draw a horse for her last weekend. She's five, and she is absolutely obsessed with horses right now. Everything is ponies and unicorns. We got her this little pink rocking horse for her birthday, and she practically lives on the thing. It has this little button on the ear that makes a neighing sound when you press it. Although, the battery is starting to die, so now it sounds more like a frog croaking than a horse. It's actually pretty funny. I tried to replace the battery, but the compartment is screwed shut with one of those tiny little Phillips head screws, and I couldn't find my precision screwdriver set. I know I put it in the top drawer of the workbench in the garage, but my son borrowed some tools a few weeks ago to fix his lawnmower, and you know how it is—things never make it back to where they belong. He's always been like that, even when he was a teenager. His room was a disaster zone. I used to tell him he was going to find a new species of mold growing under his bed one day... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Drawing? Oh, right, the medical tests. No, no doctor has ever given me a memory test or made me draw anything. Just the regular physical stuff.\n\nDoctor: Since you have been noticing these memory lapses, have you found that you are unable to complete your daily chores or personal tasks as effectively as you used to, even if you are still technically able to do them?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm unable to do them. I mean, I still get everything done around the house. The chores get finished, eventually. Like yesterday, I was supposed to be doing the laundry. I got the whites sorted out and put them in the wash, but then I noticed the washing machine was making this... this awful squeaking noise during the spin cycle. It sounded just like the belt on my old Ford pickup right before it snapped on the highway back in '98. I was stuck on the side of I-95 for three hours waiting for a tow truck. The guy who finally showed up was this huge guy named Tiny, which I always think is such a funny nickname for a big guy. He told me he used to be a professional wrestler on the local circuit before he blew out his knee. Said his finishing move was called the \"Tiny Terror.\" Anyway, I started looking for my toolbox to see if I could tighten the belt on the washer, and that's when I realized I still hadn't found that precision screwdriver set from the other day. I spent a good hour tearing the garage apart looking for it. Found a whole box of old Christmas ornaments my wife thought we lost five years ago, though. The ones with the little hand-painted snowmen. She was so happy when I brought those in... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Oh, the chores. Right. No, I'm still doing all my chores and tasks just fine. It might take me a little longer sometimes because I get sidetracked, but I still get them all done. I'm not unable to do them or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently able to manage your daily life and household chores entirely on your own, or have you reached a point where you need someone else's help or supervision to get things done safely and correctly?\n\nPatient: No, I don't need anyone's help. I can manage everything on my own just fine. I still do all the cooking and the cleaning, and I take care of the yard. Well, I mean, the yard is a bit of a project right now. I was out there the other day trying to trim the hedges along the fence line, but my electric clippers gave out on me. Just completely died. They were my father-in-law's originally, so they're probably older than I am. He bought them at this little hardware store downtown that closed back in the eighties. It was one of those places that smelled like sawdust and motor oil, you know? They had everything in there. You could walk in looking for a specific size of wingnut, and the owner, old Mr. Henderson, would know exactly which dusty little drawer to pull it from. He always wore these thick suspenders and had a cigar stub clamped in his teeth, even though he never actually lit it. My wife said he used to give her butterscotch candies when she was a little girl. I actually love butterscotch. I used to buy those little hard candies wrapped in the yellow cellophane, but my dentist told me I had to stop because I cracked a molar on one last year. That was a nightmare. Had to get a crown, and the temporary one fell out while I was eating a piece of toast...\n\nWait, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, about needing help. Right. No, no, I manage just fine on my own. I don't need any supervision or anything like that. I get it all done.\n\nDoctor: If you were to take a standardized cognitive test, such as remembering a list of words or counting backward, do you think you would have difficulty performing those tasks successfully?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'd have a problem with that. Counting backward or remembering words? Sure, I could do that. I mean, my memory isn't what it used to be, but I'm not totally gone. I remember back in high school, Mr. Peterson—he was my math teacher, real strict guy, always wore these short-sleeved button-down shirts with a tie, even in the dead of winter. Anyway, he used to make us do these mental math drills where we had to count backward from a thousand by sevens. If you messed up, you had to stay after class and clean the chalkboards. I hated cleaning those things. The chalk dust would get everywhere, all over your clothes, in your hair. I used to have this dark blue corduroy jacket I wore every day, and it would just get covered in white dust. My mom was always getting onto me about it. She was a stickler for keeping things neat. She used to iron everything, even my dad's underwear, if you can believe that. He worked at the lumber mill, so his clothes were always getting ruined anyway, but she insisted... \n\nWait, what was the question? A test? Oh, yeah, no, I'm sure I'd do fine on a test like that. I don't think I'd have any trouble."
